<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme for counting common integer bases of two polymatroids with the same total rank, supplied by exact rank-value oracles. The total rank and capacities are encoded in binary, and each integer vector is counted once. On every execution, the number of oracle calls and the bit work outside the oracles are bounded by a fixed polynomial in the ground-set size, the binary input length, the inverse relative-error tolerance, and the logarithm of the inverse failure probability. The algorithm handles binary capacities directly, without expanding them into labelled copies.

- **claim-main-fpras** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a single uniform randomized algorithm which, for every input described above, outputs a nonnegative rational $\widehat Z$ satisfying $$\mathbb P\bigl((1-\varepsilon)Z\le\widehat Z\le(1+\varepsilon)Z\bigr) \ge1-\delta.$$ If $Z=0$, the algorithm outputs zero on every execution. On every execution, both its number of oracle calls and its number of other bit operations are bounded by one fixed polynomial in $$n,\qquad \ell,\qquad \varepsilon^{-1},\qquad \log(\delta^{-1}).$$ The bit-operation bound includes rational arithmetic, generation of independent unbiased random bits, and the encoding of the output.
  - Explanation: A single uniform randomized algorithm approximates the number of common integer bases within the stated relative error and failure probability, returns zero when there are none, and has oracle and bit costs bounded by one fixed polynomial in the stated parameters.
- **claim-oracle-tools** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-oracle-tools)): The following operations have deterministic oracle and bit complexity bounded by fixed polynomials in the indicated input sizes.* 1. *An integer-valued submodular function on a finite ground set can be minimized, with a minimizing set returned, from an exact value oracle and a bound on the binary lengths of its values. The size parameters are the ground-set cardinality and that value-length bound.* 2. *Let $Q\subseteq\mathbb R^d$ be a bounded rational polytope, supplied by strong separation, a coordinate bound, and a bound on the binary encoding length of each individual defining inequality. The separator returns inequalities of length polynomial in the query length and the supplied description bounds. Exact rational linear optimization, including an emptiness decision and the production of an optimal vertex when $Q$ is nonempty, is polynomial in $d$, these bounds, and the objective length. This assertion includes lower-dimensional polytopes. All separation queries have polynomial encoding length.* *The first operation also applies to an integer submodular function with a rational modular term added, and with prescribed elements required to belong or not to belong to the minimizing set.
  - Explanation: Submodular minimization from a value oracle and exact rational linear optimization over a well-described polytope given by strong separation both have deterministic polynomial oracle and bit cost in the size parameters, so binary capacities cause no dependence on their numerical value.
- **claim-base-calculus** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-base-calculus)): Let $f,g:2^E\to\mathbb R$ be normalized submodular functions and let $v\in\mathbb R^E$.* 1. *If $w_{\pi(1)}\geq\cdots\geq w_{\pi(n)}$ and $A_k=\{\pi(1),\ldots,\pi(k)\}$, the vector $$b_{\pi(k)}=f(A_k)-f(A_{k-1})$$ belongs to $B(f)$ and maximizes $w\cdot x$ there.* 2. *With $(f+v)(A)=f(A)+v(A)$ and $f^{\mathrm{ref}}(A)=f(E\setminus A)-f(E)$, one has $$B(f+g)=B(f)+B(g),\qquad B(f+v)=B(f)+v,\qquad B(f^{\mathrm{ref}})=-B(f).$$* 3. *For a partition $\mathcal D$ of $E$, define $\pi_{\mathcal D}(x)=(x(D):D\in\mathcal D)$ and $\bar f(\mathcal J)=f(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal J}D)$. Then $\pi_{\mathcal D}(B(f))=B(\bar f)$.* 4. *For distinct $a,b\in E$ and $t\geq0$, the segment $[-t,t](e_a-e_b)$ is $B(q_{ab})$, where $$q_{ab}(A)=t\,\|\mathbf1_A(a)-\mathbf1_A(b)\|.$$ In particular, adding any collection of these segments to a base polytope again gives a base polytope, with integral rank if $f$ and the segment lengths are integral.
  - Explanation: Greedy vectors maximize linear functionals on a base polytope, and sums, translations, reflections and partition projections of submodular functions correspond to Minkowski sums, shifts, negations and projections of base polytopes; adding segments keeps a base polytope.
- **claim-expansion-matroid** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-expansion-matroid)): Let $r:2^E\to\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$ be normalized, nondecreasing, and submodular. Replace each $j\in E$ by any finite number of labelled copies. Declare a set of copies independent when its occupancy vector $z$ satisfies $z(A)\leq r(A)$ for every $A\subseteq E$. This defines a matroid. A set of copies with occupancy vector $v$ has matroid rank $$\label{eq:expansion-rank} m(v)=\min_{A\subseteq E}\{r(A)+v(E\setminus A)\}.$$
  - Explanation: Replacing each element by labelled copies and calling a copy set independent when its occupancy vector respects the rank function gives a matroid whose rank is a minimum over subsets; it is used only as a proof device.
- **claim-polyhedral-controls** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-polyhedral-controls)): Let $Q\subseteq\mathbb R^E$ be nonempty and be defined either by $B(f_1)\cap B(f_2)$ or by $B(f)$ together with equations $x(D)=a_D$ for the parts $D$ of a partition of $E$. The functions are normalized and submodular. In either case arbitrary coordinate lower and upper bounds may also be imposed.* 1. *If the ranks and all other right sides are integral, every vertex of $Q$ is integral.* 2. *Suppose $x\in\mathbb R^E$ satisfies every defining inequality with additive violation at most $b\geq0$, and every defining equality with absolute error at most $b$. Then $$\label{eq:polyhedral-distance} \operatorname{dist}_{\infty}(x,Q)\leq10(n+1)^2b.$$* 3. *Fix $a\in Q$ and an affine function $F$ whose linear part is integral. The function $$\phi(l)=\max\{F(y):y\in Q,\ \\|y-a\\|_\infty\leq l\}, \qquad l\geq0,$$ is continuous, nondecreasing, concave, and piecewise affine. Every positive slope of an affine piece is an integer and hence at least one. Eventually $\phi(l)=\max_Q F$. Consequently, if $F\geq0$ on $Q$ and $\phi(L)<L$ for some $L>0$, then $\phi(L)=\max_QF$.
  - Explanation: For the intersection of two base polytopes or a base polytope with part-total equations, vertices are integral for integral data, approximate points are close to the polytope in sup norm with a bound polynomial in the dimension, and the radius-restricted optimum is concave piecewise affine with integer slopes.
- **claim-center** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-center)): Let $Q\subseteq\mathbb R^n$ be a nonempty bounded rational polytope satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:oracle-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:oracle-tools">2</a>. One can find a rational $c\in Q$, with polynomial encoding length, such that every affine $F$ nonnegative on $Q$ satisfies $$\label{eq:center-slack} \max_{x\in Q}F(x)\leq4(n+1)F(c).$$ The construction has polynomial oracle and bit complexity. It also returns an affinely spanning vertex simplex of $Q$ such that every point of $Q$ has all barycentric coordinates of absolute value at most two with respect to that simplex.
  - Explanation: A rational center of polynomial encoding length can be built by determinant replacement so that the maximum of any nonnegative affine function is controlled by its value at the center; an affinely spanning vertex simplex with bounded barycentric coordinates is also returned.
- **claim-balanced-summation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-balanced-summation)): Let $F>0$ on the entire slice $\mathcal B(m,r)$ and suppose it has the removal signature. Choose disjoint coordinate pairs $\{a_t,b_t\}$ with $m_{a_t}=m_{b_t}=M_t\ge1$. Their balanced sum $$\Phi(z)=\sum_{0\le k_t\le M_t} F\bigl(z,(k_t,M_t-k_t)_t\bigr)$$ is strictly positive on the entire remaining box slice of total $r-\sum_tM_t$ and has the removal signature. The same statement holds with “addition” in place of “removal”. Fixing coordinates at prescribed box-valid values preserves either signature and positivity on the resulting valid slice.
  - Explanation: Summing paired coordinates at balance keeps strict positivity and the removal (or addition) signature of a positive weight, and fixing coordinates preserves them as well.
- **claim-rank-signatures** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rank-signatures)): Partition a set of count coordinates $I$ into groups indexed by $E$, allowing empty groups, and let $b\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^E$. For either of the two consistent orientations $$v_e=b_e+\sum_{i\text{ in group }e}z_i, \qquad\text{or}\qquad v_e=b_e+\sum_{i\text{ in group }e}(m_i-z_i),$$ the factor $G(z)=\Phi_{r,H}(v)$ satisfies $\mathbf 1\mathbf 1^{\mathsf T}-Q\succeq0$ for both signs in <a href="#eq:signature-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:signature-normalization">[eq:signature-normalization]</a>. The product of such factors on disjoint sets of count coordinates has both integer signatures on every total slice. The assertion holds for every nonnegative power of the product, and for any coordinates fixed at box-valid values.* *More generally, a base constraint for an arbitrary integral normalized submodular rank, with a fixed integer translation and either consistent orientation of its grouped arguments on a finite box, can be imposed as a limit of factors of this kind.
  - Explanation: Soft rank-penalty factors on grouped count coordinates satisfy the normalized positive-semidefinite tests for additions and removals in both orientations, products and nonnegative powers keep both integer signatures, and general submodular base constraints arise as limits.
- **claim-shift-logconcavity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-shift-logconcavity)): Let $f_1,f_2$ be integral normalized submodular functions on $E$, not necessarily nondecreasing, and define $$Y(d)=\#\{(x_1,x_2)\in(B(f_1)\cap\mathbb Z^E)\times (B(f_2)\cap\mathbb Z^E):x_1-x_2=d\}.$$ For $d_0\in\mathbb Z^E$ and distinct $j,k\in E$, put $y(t)=Y(d_0+t(e_j-e_k))$, $t\in\mathbb Z$. The positive support of $y$ is an integer interval, possibly empty or a singleton. For integers $a<t<b$ with $y(a),y(b)>0$, $$y(t)\ge y(a)^{(b-t)/(b-a)}y(b)^{(t-a)/(b-a)}. \label{eq:shift-interpolation}$$
  - Explanation: Along an elementary root direction the count of pairs of integer base points with a given difference has interval support and satisfies the secant log-concavity inequality, obtained as a limit of positive approximations with both signatures.
- **claim-generator-prefixes** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-generator-prefixes)): Let $\mathcal A=(A_1,\ldots,A_m)$ be a family of subsets of $E$. Its atoms admit an order such that every prefix is a union of at most $n$ intersections of members of $\mathcal A$, allowing $E$ as the empty intersection. If $s$ is normalized, nonnegative, and submodular, $s(E)=0$, and $s(A_j)\le b$ for every $j$, where $b\ge0$, then in this order every prefix $F$ satisfies $$s(F)\le n(m+1)b.$$ Consequently, suppose that $Q\subseteq B(r)$ and $r(A_j)-x(A_j)\le b$ for all $x\in Q$ and all $j$. For every atom $D$ of $\mathcal A$ and every $x,y\in Q$, $$\label{eq:part-range} \|x(D)-y(D)\|\le 2n(m+1)b.$$
  - Explanation: The atoms of a short generating family can be ordered so that every prefix has small submodular slack, and consequently all atom totals vary by a controlled amount over a set of points with bounded generator slack.
- **claim-partitions** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-partitions)): Each part of $\mathcal D_i$ is contained in a superblock. The three partitions can be found with polynomially many oracle calls and bit operations. Each has a generating family of at most $n^2$ sets satisfying its defining strict slack bound.* *Fix a superblock order $G_1,\ldots,G_g$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:generator-prefixes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:generator-prefixes">11</a> for the $s_*$-generators. Every prefix $F_k=G_1\cup\cdots\cup G_k$, including the empty and full prefixes, satisfies $$\label{eq:superblock-prefix-slack} s_*(F_k)\le N^4S.$$ For system 1 use this order, and for system 2 use its reverse. The sets preceding a superblock $G_k$ in these orders are therefore $$\label{eq:superblock-prefixes} A_{1G_k}=F_{k-1},\qquad A_{2G_k}=E\setminus F_k.$$
  - Explanation: The fine parts refine the superblocks, all three partitions are computable with polynomially many oracle calls and bit operations from short generating families, and superblock prefixes have bounded slack in compatible orders for the two systems.
- **claim-shift-average** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-shift-average)): For $F=Y$ and for $F=Y^+$, every integral full or restricted star shift with $\|l_j\|\le S/N^{25}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:small-shift-ratio} e^{-N^{-8}}\le \frac{F(d(l))}{F(0)}\le e^{N^{-8}}.$$ For $(\kappa,A_\kappa)=(\omega,A_\omega)$ or $(\psi,J)$, on its corresponding star lattice, $$\label{eq:average-comparison} e^{-3N^{-8}}F(0) \le \frac{1}{A_\kappa}\sum_l\kappa(l)F(d(l)) \le e^{3N^{-8}}F(0).$$ The lower bound remains valid if the sum is restricted to $\max_j\|l_j\|\le S/N^{25}$.
  - Explanation: Small star shifts change the difference count by a negligible relative factor, so normalized kernel averages over shifts approximate the unshifted count, and the lower bound survives restriction to small shifts.
- **claim-refinement-witnesses** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-refinement-witnesses)): Fix integer $w$ for which both $Q_i(w)$ are nonempty. For each system separately, its lattice points and those of its expanded slice can be partitioned by fixing integer totals on successively finer parts. Each resulting original slice has the form $$Q=\{x\in B(r_i):x(D)=v_D\ (D\in\mathcal P)\},$$ where $\mathcal P$ refines $\mathcal G$ and every part of $\mathcal P$ is a union of $\mathcal D_i$-parts. Its expanded partner is exactly $Q+\Delta_i$. Every nonempty terminal slice has a point $a^*\in Q$ satisfying $$\begin{aligned} \\|a^*-c\\|_\infty&\le N^{60}S, \label{eq:witness-distance}\\ r_i(A)-a^*(A)&\ge S &&\text{if \(A\) cuts a part of \(\mathcal P\)}, \label{eq:witness-terminal-slack}\\ r_i(A)-a^*(A)&\ge W/2 &&\text{if \(A\) cuts a part of \(\mathcal D_i\)}. \label{eq:witness-fine-slack} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Each system's slices can be subdivided by fixing totals on successively finer parts so that every terminal slice has a witness point near the center with large slack on all cutting sets; this is an existence argument and is never executed.
- **claim-expansion-cost** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-expansion)): The expanded common-base count satisfies $$\label{eq:expansion-comparison} Z\le Z^+\le e^{20N^{-8}}Z.$$
  - Explanation: Enlarging both base polytopes along the fine-part directions increases the common-base count by at most a factor close to one.
- **claim-tree-coordinates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-tree-coordinates)): There is a unique pair $X_i(u,z)$ with these properties and $X_{1e}=z_e$ on nontree edges. Its coordinates are integral linear functions of $(u,z)$, with coefficients of absolute value at most $10(n+1)$. Each coordinate depends only on variables in its superblock. For integral $(u,z)$ this is a bijection onto integral pairs with the specified $\mathcal D_1$-totals for $X_1$ and $\mathcal D_2$-totals for $X_2$, and with $X_1-X_2$ supported on the representatives.
  - Explanation: Spanning-tree elimination expresses all coordinates as integral linear functions of the part totals and free coordinates with bounded coefficients, giving a bijection on integral points with no multiplicity.
- **claim-port-penalties** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-port-penalties)): An original base pair with a representative shift satisfies <a href="#eq:hard-port-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hard-port-conditions">[eq:hard-port-conditions]</a> and $\sum_GV_G\le N^7S$. Conversely, if <a href="#eq:hard-port-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hard-port-conditions">[eq:hard-port-conditions]</a> holds, then $$X_i(A)-r_i(A)\le\sum_G V_G\qquad(A\subseteq E, i=1,2).$$ If in addition $\sum_GV_G\le T$, then $X_i\in B_i^+$ for both $i$, where $B_i^+$ is defined in <a href="#eq:expanded-bases" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:expanded-bases">[eq:expanded-bases]</a>.
  - Explanation: Original base pairs with a representative shift satisfy the hard port conditions with bounded penalty, and conversely the penalties bound every rank violation and place the point in the expanded polytopes when the total penalty is small.
- **claim-smooth-derivatives** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-smooth-derivatives)): In block count coordinates, $g=\log\widetilde I_G$ is $C^3$, is concave, and satisfies, everywhere, $$\label{eq:smooth-derivative-bounds} \|g''_{ij}\|\le A_2=N^{800}/W^2,\qquad \|g'''_{ijk}\|\le A_3=N^{1250}/W^3.$$
  - Explanation: After uniform-kernel smoothing, the logarithm of the block integral is three times continuously differentiable and concave, with explicit polynomially controlled bounds on second and third derivatives.
- **claim-block-signatures** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-block-signatures)): For $0\le s\le1$, every block factor $F_G^s$ has both normalized positive-semidefinite tests of <a href="#eq:signature-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:signature-normalization">[eq:signature-normalization]</a>: $\mathbf 1\mathbf 1^{\mathsf T}-Q\succeq0$, for additions and removals.
  - Explanation: The strictly concave quadratic term dominates the discretization errors, so every power of a block factor passes both normalized positive-semidefinite tests.
- **claim-port-model** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-port-model)): For every nonempty input after the deterministic feasibility test, the preceding construction gives at most $2n$ paired ports of lengths $d_{iD}\le N^{21}W$, with $p=\sum d_{iD}$ polynomial in $N$, and a strictly positive weight $h$ on the entire slice <a href="#eq:paired-count-slice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:paired-count-slice">[eq:paired-count-slice]</a>. For every real $s\in[0,1]$, $h^s$ has both integer signatures of Definition <a href="#def:integer-signatures" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:integer-signatures">7</a>. Its balanced mass $$\mathcal Z_{\rm port} =\sum_{\substack{(a,b)\in\mathcal S\\ a_{iD}+b_{iD}=d_{iD}\ \forall(i,D)}}h(a,b)$$ satisfies $\|\log(\mathcal Z_{\rm port}/(JZ))\|\le100N^{-7}$. These statements include $p=0$, when there is a single profile.
  - Explanation: The construction yields polynomially many short paired ports and a positive weight on the whole slice whose every power in the unit interval has both integer signatures and whose balanced mass approximates the target count.
- **claim-weight-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-weight-bounds)): For every block and every port argument in its box, $$\label{eq:integral-bounds} e^{-N^{50000}}\le\widetilde I_G(u)\le e^K.$$ For the weight $h$ on the full paired slice of Proposition <a href="#prop:port-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:port-model">20</a>, $$\label{eq:weight-log-bound} \|\log h(a,b)\|\le N^{60000}.$$ All rational coefficients needed to define or evaluate its factors have polynomial encoding length in the input and in the query.
  - Explanation: Smoothed block integrals and the point weight have two-sided magnitude bounds independent of the numerical value of the capacity, and all rational coefficients have polynomial encoding length.
- **claim-volume-lift** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-volume-lift)): The polytope <a href="#eq:volume-lift" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:volume-lift">[eq:volume-lift]</a> has dimension $f+6m+d_0$, a polynomial facet-encoding bound, and an exact polynomial-time strong separation oracle. It contains a known rational ball of radius $1/32$ and has an outer ball of polynomial encoding length. Furthermore, $$\label{eq:volume-lift-accuracy} (1-\rho/10)\widetilde I_G(u) \le\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}(\mathcal Q)\le\widetilde I_G(u).$$ All polynomial bounds are in the original input length, the encoding lengths of $u$ and $\rho$, $N$, and $\rho^{-1}$, with absolute fixed exponents.
  - Explanation: The lifted polytope has the stated dimension, polynomial facet encoding, an exact strong separation oracle and explicit inner and outer balls, and its volume approximates the smoothed block integral within a small relative error.
- **claim-weight-evaluator** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-prop-weight-evaluator)): Given a profile in the entire slice $\mathcal S$, rational $0<\rho,\beta<1$, and a rational power $s\in[0,1]$ with denominator $q_s$, there is an evaluator using fresh random bits and returning a strictly positive rational $\widehat h_s$ such that $$\mathbb P\bigl[(1-\rho)h^s\le\widehat h_s\le(1+\rho)h^s\bigr] \ge1-\beta.$$ Its output length, number of rank queries, and other bit operations are bounded on every execution by fixed polynomials in $N$, the input encoding lengths, $\rho^{-1}$, $\log(\beta^{-1})$, and $q_s$. In particular an equally spaced schedule with a polynomial number of phases has polynomially bounded denominators. Moreover $J$ admits a deterministic positive rational approximation $\widehat J$ with $\|\log(\widehat J/J)\|\le N^{-7}$ in polynomial work.
  - Explanation: A randomized evaluator with capped resources returns a positive rational approximation of the weight power with the stated accuracy and confidence, and a deterministic rational approximation of the normalizer is available.
- **claim-counting-lifts** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-counting-lifts)): If $k$ has both integer signatures of Definition <a href="#def:integer-signatures" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:integer-signatures">7</a>, then $f$ has the omission property and $\widetilde f$ has the addition property. Both properties persist under fixing included or excluded elements and summing disjoint labelled pairs at occupancy one, on each resulting full valid-degree domain. Moreover, with $q=(p+1)^2$, $$q^{-1}f(S)\leq\widetilde f(S)\leq qf(S) \quad\left(S\in\mathcal A\cup\bigcup_{i\ne l}\mathcal A_{il}\right). \label{eq:counting-comparison}$$
  - Explanation: If the count weight has both integer signatures, the two factorial lifts have the omission and addition properties respectively, these persist under fixing and summing pairs, and the lifts are comparable on transversals and single-defect classes.
- **claim-counting-transport** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-counting-transport)): Suppose $f$ is positive on all $p$-subsets and has the omission property, $\widetilde f$ is positive on that domain and has the addition property, the weights agree on $\mathcal A$, and <a href="#eq:counting-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:counting-comparison">[eq:counting-comparison]</a> holds with $q\geq1$. For good multipliers put $$C_{\rm p}=p(1+8pq^2),\qquad C_{\rm d}=8+32pq^3,\qquad K_{\rm obs}=10p^4(9C_{\rm p}+2C_{\rm d}). \label{eq:counting-constants}$$ Then for every real function $g$ on $\mathcal X$, $$C\operatorname{Var}_\mu(g)\leq C_{\rm p}\mathfrak D(g),\qquad \operatorname{Var}_\pi(\mathcal Qg) \leq(9C_{\rm p}+2C_{\rm d})\frac{\mathfrak D(g)}C \leq K_{\rm obs}\mathcal E_P(g). \label{eq:counting-variance}$$ If $\mathcal QG=G$ and an initial law satisfies $\nu\leq D\pi$ pointwise, then $m\geq1$ consecutive observations satisfy $$\mathbb E_\nu\left[ \left(m^{-1}\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}G(S_t)-\mathbb E_\pi G\right)^2\right] \leq\frac{2D K_{\rm obs}}m\operatorname{Var}_\pi(G). \label{eq:counting-time-average}$$ The trace on $\mathcal A$, counting every strictly positive return as one step, is lazy and reversible with stationary law $\mu$ and inverse spectral gap at most $2p^2C_{\rm p}\leq K_{\rm obs}$. In particular, $$\left\|\frac{P_{\rm tr}^t(x,y)}{\mu(y)}-1\right\| \leq\mu_{\min}^{-1}\exp(-t/K_{\rm obs}). \label{eq:counting-trace-mixing}$$ If a trace starts from $\nu\leq D\mu$, the expected cost of $t$ trace steps in underlying transitions is at most $tD/\pi(\mathcal A)$; at each deterministic trace index its law is still at most $D\mu$.
  - Explanation: Under the stated positivity, signature and comparison hypotheses and good multipliers, observable variance bounds, a time-average bound and trace mixing hold, imported from the companion transport framework.
- **claim-counting-initialization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-counting-initialization)): For every $p$-subset and $0\leq j<H_o$, $$\frac12\leq\frac{f_{j+1}(S)}{f_j(S)}\leq1. \label{eq:counting-adjacent}$$ Thus $C_j/c_{il}(j)$ changes by a factor in $[1/2,2]$ between consecutive phases, and $\mu_j=f_j/C_j$ on $\mathcal A$ satisfies $\mu_j\leq2\mu_{j+1}$. Moreover $$\min_{S\in\mathcal A}\mu_j(S) \geq2^{-(H_o+p+K_o)}. \label{eq:counting-minimum}$$ The initial masses are exact rational numbers computable in polynomial bit time, and $\mu_0$ can be sampled by independent uniform port counts followed by uniform subsets of the pairs realizing those counts.
  - Explanation: Adjacent annealing phases have comparable weights, the minimum transversal probability has an explicit lower bound, and the initial masses are exact and the initial law is directly samplable.
- **claim-counting-reference** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-counting-reference)): With probability at least $1-1/50$, this experiment does not abort, every stored multiplier collection is good at its own phase, and $$\left\|\log\frac{Y_{\rm ref}}{C_{H_o}}\right\|\leq4H_o\eta. \label{eq:counting-reference-error}$$
  - Explanation: With high probability the capped annealing experiment does not abort, all stored multipliers stay good, and the product of ratio estimates approximates the final transversal mass with small logarithmic error.
- **claim-counting-bits** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-counting-bits)): The implemented experiment has deterministic polynomial bounds on oracle calls, bit operations, and output length, on every execution. It can be coupled to the capped reference experiment so that, except with probability at most $20J_*\xi$, all states, type counts, multiplier updates, and cap decisions coincide, every weight evaluation meets its tolerance, and each positive observed ratio differs from its reference value by log error at most $4\xi$.
  - Explanation: The fair-bit implementation has deterministic polynomial resource bounds on every execution and can be coupled to the capped reference experiment with small failure probability.

      "exact_statement": "Let $f,g:2^E\\to\\mathbb R$ be normalized submodular functions and let $v\\in\\mathbb R^E$.*\n\n1.  *If $w_{\\pi(1)}\\geq\\cdots\\geq w_{\\pi(n)}$ and $A_k=\\{\\pi(1),\\ldots,\\pi(k)\\}$, the vector $$b_{\\pi(k)}=f(A_k)-f(A_{k-1})$$ belongs to $B(f)$ and maximizes $w\\cdot x$ there.*\n\n2.  *With $(f+v)(A)=f(A)+v(A)$ and $f^{\\mathrm{ref}}(A)=f(E\\setminus A)-f(E)$, one has $$B(f+g)=B(f)+B(g),\\qquad B(f+v)=B(f)+v,\\qquad\n     B(f^{\\mathrm{ref}})=-B(f).$$*\n\n3.  *For a partition $\\mathcal D$ of $E$, define $\\pi_{\\mathcal D}(x)=(x(D):D\\in\\mathcal D)$ and $\\bar f(\\mathcal J)=f(\\bigcup_{D\\in\\mathcal J}D)$. Then $\\pi_{\\mathcal D}(B(f))=B(\\bar f)$.*\n\n4.  *For distinct $a,b\\in E$ and $t\\geq0$, the segment $[-t,t](e_a-e_b)$ is $B(q_{ab})$, where $$q_{ab}(A)=t\\,|\\mathbf1_A(a)-\\mathbf1_A(b)|.$$ In particular, adding any collection of these segments to a base polytope again gives a base polytope, with integral rank if $f$ and the segment lengths are integral.",
      "explanation": "Greedy vectors maximize linear functionals on a base polytope, and sums, translations, reflections and partition projections of submodular functions correspond to Minkowski sums, shifts, negations and projections of base polytopes; adding segments keeps a base polytope.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",

        }

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-expansion-matroid",
        "exact_quote": "Let $r:2^E\\to\\mathbb Z_{\\geq0}$ be normalized, nondecreasing, and submodular. Replace each $j\\in E$ by any finite number of labelled copies. Declare a set of copies independent when its occupancy vector $z$ satisfies $z(A)\\leq r(A)$ for every $A\\subseteq E$. This defines a matroid. A set of copies with occupancy vector $v$ has matroid rank $$\\label{eq:expansion-rank}\n m(v)=\\min_{A\\subseteq E}\\{r(A)+v(E\\setminus A)\\}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

        }

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-polyhedral-controls",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Q\\subseteq\\mathbb R^E$ be nonempty and be defined either by $B(f_1)\\cap B(f_2)$ or by $B(f)$ together with equations $x(D)=a_D$ for the parts $D$ of a partition of $E$. The functions are normalized and submodular. In either case arbitrary coordinate lower and upper bounds may also be imposed.*\n\n1.  *If the ranks and all other right sides are integral, every vertex of $Q$ is integral.*\n\n\n2.  *Suppose $x\\in\\mathbb R^E$ satisfies every defining inequality with additive violation at most $b\\geq0$, and every defining equality with absolute error at most $b$. Then $$\\label{eq:polyhedral-distance}\n      \\operatorname{dist}_{\\infty}(x,Q)\\leq10(n+1)^2b.$$*\n\n3.  *Fix $a\\in Q$ and an affine function $F$ whose linear part is integral. The function $$\\phi(l)=\\max\\{F(y):y\\in Q,\\ \\|y-a\\|_\\infty\\leq l\\},\n      \\qquad l\\geq0,$$ is continuous, nondecreasing, concave, and piecewise affine. Every positive slope of an affine piece is an integer and hence at least one. Eventually $\\phi(l)=\\max_Q F$. Consequently, if $F\\geq0$ on $Q$ and $\\phi(L)<L$ for some $L>0$, then $\\phi(L)=\\max_QF$.",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-center",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Q\\subseteq\\mathbb R^n$ be a nonempty bounded rational polytope satisfying the hypotheses of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:oracle-tools\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:oracle-tools\">2</a>. One can find a rational $c\\in Q$, with polynomial encoding length, such that every affine $F$ nonnegative on $Q$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:center-slack}\n \\max_{x\\in Q}F(x)\\leq4(n+1)F(c).$$ The construction has polynomial oracle and bit complexity. It also returns an affinely spanning vertex simplex of $Q$ such that every point of $Q$ has all barycentric coordinates of absolute value at most two with respect to that simplex.",

      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-balanced-summation",
        "exact_quote": "Let $F>0$ on the entire slice $\\mathcal B(m,r)$ and suppose it has the removal signature. Choose disjoint coordinate pairs $\\{a_t,b_t\\}$ with $m_{a_t}=m_{b_t}=M_t\\ge1$. Their balanced sum $$\\Phi(z)=\\sum_{0\\le k_t\\le M_t}\n F\\bigl(z,(k_t,M_t-k_t)_t\\bigr)$$ is strictly positive on the entire remaining box slice of total $r-\\sum_tM_t$ and has the removal signature. The same statement holds with “addition” in place of “removal”. Fixing coordinates at prescribed box-valid values preserves either signature and positivity on the resulting valid slice.",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-signatures",
        "exact_quote": "Partition a set of count coordinates $I$ into groups indexed by $E$, allowing empty groups, and let $b\\in\\mathbb Z_{\\ge0}^E$. For either of the two consistent orientations $$v_e=b_e+\\sum_{i\\text{ in group }e}z_i,\n \\qquad\\text{or}\\qquad\n v_e=b_e+\\sum_{i\\text{ in group }e}(m_i-z_i),$$ the factor $G(z)=\\Phi_{r,H}(v)$ satisfies $\\mathbf 1\\mathbf 1^{\\mathsf T}-Q\\succeq0$ for both signs in <a href=\"#eq:signature-normalization\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:signature-normalization\">[eq:signature-normalization]</a>. The product of such factors on disjoint sets of count coordinates has both integer signatures on every total slice. The assertion holds for every nonnegative power of the product, and for any coordinates fixed at box-valid values.*\n\n*More generally, a base constraint for an arbitrary integral normalized submodular rank, with a fixed integer translation and either consistent orientation of its grouped arguments on a finite box, can be imposed as a limit of factors of this kind.",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-rank-signatures",
          "exact_quote": "Partition a set of count coordinates $I$ into groups indexed by $E$, allowing empty groups, and let $b\\in\\mathbb Z_{\\ge0}^E$. The product of such factors on disjoint sets of count coordinates has both integer signatures on every total slice. The assertion holds for every nonnegative power of the product, and for any coordinates fixed at box-valid values.*\n\n*More generally, a base constraint for an arbitrary integral normalized submodular rank, with a fixed integer translation and either consistent orientation of its grouped arguments on a finite box, can be imposed as a limit of factors of this kind.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-shift-logconcavity",
        "exact_quote": "Let $f_1,f_2$ be integral normalized submodular functions on $E$, not necessarily nondecreasing, and define $$Y(d)=\\#\\{(x_1,x_2)\\in(B(f_1)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E)\\times\n                         (B(f_2)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E):x_1-x_2=d\\}.$$ For $d_0\\in\\mathbb Z^E$ and distinct $j,k\\in E$, put $y(t)=Y(d_0+t(e_j-e_k))$, $t\\in\\mathbb Z$. The positive support of $y$ is an integer interval, possibly empty or a singleton. For integers $a<t<b$ with $y(a),y(b)>0$, $$y(t)\\ge y(a)^{(b-t)/(b-a)}y(b)^{(t-a)/(b-a)}.\n \\label{eq:shift-interpolation}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-shift-logconcavity",
          "exact_quote": "Let $f_1,f_2$ be integral normalized submodular functions on $E$, not necessarily nondecreasing, and define $$Y(d)=\\#\\{(x_1,x_2)\\in(B(f_1)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E)\\times\n                         (B(f_2)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E):x_1-x_2=d\\}.$$ For $d_0\\in\\mathbb Z^E$ and distinct $j,k\\in E$, put $y(t)=Y(d_0+t(e_j-e_k))$, $t\\in\\mathbb Z$.         }

      "exact_statement": "Let $f_1,f_2$ be integral normalized submodular functions on $E$, not necessarily nondecreasing, and define $$Y(d)=\\#\\{(x_1,x_2)\\in(B(f_1)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E)\\times\n                         (B(f_2)\\cap\\mathbb Z^E):x_1-x_2=d\\}.$$ For $d_0\\in\\mathbb Z^E$ and distinct $j,k\\in E$, put $y(t)=Y(d_0+t(e_j-e_k))$, $t\\in\\mathbb Z$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-generator-prefixes",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal A=(A_1,\\ldots,A_m)$ be a family of subsets of $E$. Its atoms admit an order such that every prefix is a union of at most $n$ intersections of members of $\\mathcal A$, allowing $E$ as the empty intersection. If $s$ is normalized, nonnegative, and submodular, $s(E)=0$, and $s(A_j)\\le b$ for every $j$, where $b\\ge0$, then in this order every prefix $F$ satisfies $$s(F)\\le n(m+1)b.$$ Consequently, suppose that $Q\\subseteq B(r)$ and $r(A_j)-x(A_j)\\le b$ for all $x\\in Q$ and all $j$. For every atom $D$ of $\\mathcal A$ and every $x,y\\in Q$, $$\\label{eq:part-range}\n |x(D)-y(D)|\\le 2n(m+1)b.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-partitions",
        "exact_quote": "Each part of $\\mathcal D_i$ is contained in a superblock. The three partitions can be found with polynomially many oracle calls and bit operations.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-port-model",
        "exact_quote": "For every nonempty input after the deterministic feasibility test, the preceding construction gives at most $2n$ paired ports of lengths $d_{iD}\\le N^{21}W$, with $p=\\sum d_{iD}$ polynomial in $N$, and a strictly positive weight $h$ on the entire slice <a href=\"#eq:paired-count-slice\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:paired-count-slice\">[eq:paired-count-slice]</a>.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-volume-lift",
        "exact_quote": "The polytope <a href=\"#eq:volume-lift\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:volume-lift\">[eq:volume-lift]</a> has dimension $f+6m+d_0$, a polynomial facet-encoding bound, and an exact polynomial-time strong separation oracle.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-weight-evaluator",
        "exact_quote": "Given a profile in the entire slice $\\mathcal S$, rational $0<\\rho,\\beta<1$, and a rational power $s\\in[0,1]$ with denominator $q_s$, there is an evaluator using fresh random bits and returning a strictly positive rational $\\widehat h_s$ such that $$\\mathbb P\\bigl[(1-\\rho)h^s\\le\\widehat h_s\\le(1+\\rho)h^s\\bigr]\n       \\ge1-\\beta.$$ Its output length, number of rank queries, and other bit operations are bounded on every execution by fixed polynomials in $N$, the input encoding lengths, $\\rho^{-1}$, $\\log(\\beta^{-1})$, and $q_s$. In particular an equally spaced schedule with a polynomial number of phases has polynomially bounded denominators. Moreover $J$ admits a deterministic positive rational approximation $\\widehat J$ with $|\\log(\\widehat J/J)|\\le N^{-7}$ in polynomial work.",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-weight-evaluator",
          "exact_quote": "Given a profile in the entire slice $\\mathcal S$, rational $0<\\rho,\\beta<1$, and a rational power $s\\in[0,1]$ with denominator $q_s$, there is an evaluator using fresh random bits and returning a strictly positive rational $\\widehat h_s$ such that $$\\mathbb P\\bigl[(1-\\rho)h^s\\le\\widehat h_s\\le(1+\\rho)h^s\\bigr]\n       \\ge1-\\beta.$$ Its output length, number of rank queries, and other bit operations are bounded on every execution by fixed polynomials in $N$, the input encoding lengths, $\\rho^{-1}$, $\\log(\\beta^{-1})$, and $q_s$. In particular an equally spaced schedule with a polynomial number of phases has polynomially bounded denominators. Moreover $J$ admits a deterministic positive rational approximation $\\widehat J$ with $|\\log(\\widehat J/J)|\\le N^{-7}$ in polynomial work.",
          "source_id": "PM-114.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Given a profile in the entire slice $\\mathcal S$, rational $0<\\rho,\\beta<1$, and a rational power $s\\in[0,1]$ with denominator $q_s$, there is an evaluator using fresh random bits and returning a strictly positive rational $\\widehat h_s$ such that $$\\mathbb P\\bigl[(1-\\rho)h^s\\le\\widehat h_s\\le(1+\\rho)h^s\\bigr]\n       \\ge1-\\beta.$$ Its output length, number of rank queries, and other bit operations are bounded on every execution by fixed polynomials in $N$, the input encoding lengths, $\\rho^{-1}$, $\\log(\\beta^{-1})$, and $q_s$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-counting-lifts",
        "exact_quote": "If $k$ has both integer signatures of Definition <a href=\"#def:integer-signatures\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"def:integer-signatures\">7</a>, then $f$ has the omission property and $\\widetilde f$ has the addition property. Both properties persist under fixing included or excluded elements and summing disjoint labelled pairs at occupancy one, on each resulting full valid-degree domain. Moreover, with $q=(p+1)^2$, $$q^{-1}f(S)\\leq\\widetilde f(S)\\leq qf(S)\n \\quad\\left(S\\in\\mathcal A\\cup\\bigcup_{i\\ne l}\\mathcal A_{il}\\right).\n \\label{eq:counting-comparison}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-counting-lifts",
          "exact_quote": "If $k$ has both integer signatures of Definition <a href=\"#def:integer-signatures\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"def:integer-signatures\">7</a>, then $f$ has the omission property and $\\widetilde f$ has the addition property. Moreover, with $q=(p+1)^2$, $$q^{-1}f(S)\\leq\\widetilde f(S)\\leq qf(S)\n \\quad\\left(S\\in\\mathcal A\\cup\\bigcup_{i\\ne l}\\mathcal A_{il}\\right).\n \\label{eq:counting-comparison}$$",
          "source_id": "PM-114.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "If $k$ has both integer signatures of Definition <a href=\"#def:integer-signatures\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"def:integer-signatures\">7</a>, then $f$ has the omission property and $\\widetilde f$ has the addition property. Both properties persist under fixing included or excluded elements and summing disjoint labelled pairs at occupancy one, on each resulting full valid-degree domain.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-counting-transport",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $f$ is positive on all $p$-subsets and has the omission property, $\\widetilde f$ is positive on that domain and has the addition property, the weights agree on $\\mathcal A$, and <a href=\"#eq:counting-comparison\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:counting-comparison\">[eq:counting-comparison]</a> holds with $q\\geq1$. For good multipliers put $$C_{\\rm p}=p(1+8pq^2),\\qquad\n C_{\\rm d}=8+32pq^3,\\qquad\n K_{\\rm obs}=10p^4(9C_{\\rm p}+2C_{\\rm d}).\n \\label{eq:counting-constants}$$ Then for every real function $g$ on $\\mathcal X$, $$C\\operatorname{Var}_\\mu(g)\\leq C_{\\rm p}\\mathfrak D(g),\\qquad\n \\operatorname{Var}_\\pi(\\mathcal Qg)\n \\leq(9C_{\\rm p}+2C_{\\rm d})\\frac{\\mathfrak D(g)}C\n \\leq K_{\\rm obs}\\mathcal E_P(g).\n \\label{eq:counting-variance}$$ If $\\mathcal QG=G$ and an initial law satisfies $\\nu\\leq D\\pi$ pointwise, then $m\\geq1$ consecutive observations satisfy $$\\mathbb E_\\nu\\left[\n  \\left(m^{-1}\\sum_{t=0}^{m-1}G(S_t)-\\mathbb E_\\pi G\\right)^2\\right]\n \\leq\\frac{2D K_{\\rm obs}}m\\operatorname{Var}_\\pi(G).\n \\label{eq:counting-time-average}$$ The trace on $\\mathcal A$, counting every strictly positive return as one step, is lazy and reversible with stationary law $\\mu$ and inverse spectral gap at most $2p^2C_{\\rm p}\\leq K_{\\rm obs}$. In particular, $$\\left|\\frac{P_{\\rm tr}^t(x,y)}{\\mu(y)}-1\\right|\n \\leq\\mu_{\\min}^{-1}\\exp(-t/K_{\\rm obs}).\n \\label{eq:counting-trace-mixing}$$ If a trace starts from $\\nu\\leq D\\mu$, the expected cost of $t$ trace steps in underlying transitions is at most $tD/\\pi(\\mathcal A)$; at each deterministic trace index its law is still at most $D\\mu$.",
        "source_id": "PM-114.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

In particular, $$\\left|\\frac{P_{\\rm tr}^t(x,y)}{\\mu(y)}-1\\right|\n \\leq\\mu_{\\min}^{-1}\\exp(-t/K_{\\rm obs}).\n \\label{eq:counting-trace-mixing}$$ If a trace starts from $\\nu\\leq D\\mu$, the expected cost of $t$ trace steps in underlying transitions is at most $tD/\\pi(\\mathcal A)$; at each deterministic trace index its law is still at most $D\\mu$.",

      "exact_statement": "Suppose $f$ is positive on all $p$-subsets and has the omission property, $\\widetilde f$ is positive on that domain and has the addition property, the weights agree on $\\mathcal A$, and <a href=\"#eq:counting-comparison\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:counting-comparison\">[eq:counting-comparison]</a> holds with $q\\geq1$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Integer quadratic signatures** ([source](#stmt-def-integer-signatures)): A strictly positive weight $F$ on all of $\mathcal B(m,r)$ has the *removal signature* if, for every box-valid display $z$ of total $r+2$, the matrix $$\bigl(F(z-e_i-e_j)\bigr)_{i,j\in I} \quad\text{has at most one positive eigenvalue}. \label{eq:integer-removal-test}$$ It has the *addition signature* if, for every box-valid display $z$ of total $r-2$, the same conclusion holds for $$\bigl(F(z+e_i+e_j)\bigr)_{i,j\in I}. \label{eq:integer-addition-test}$$ Diagonal entries represent two changes in the same coordinate. All invalid changes have weight zero. A test with no box-valid display is vacuous.
- **Normalized set function** ([source](#sec:polyhedra)): A set function $f:2^E\to\mathbb R$ is *normalized* if $f(\varnothing)=0$.
- **Number of common bases Z** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Write $Z=\lvert\Omega(r_1,r_2)\rvert$.
- **Subset sum notation** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For $x\in\mathbb R^E$ and $A\subseteq E$, put $x(A)=\sum_{j\in A}x_j$.
- **Atoms of a family** ([source](#pm-h-16)): The atoms of a family of subsets of $E$ are the nonempty classes of elements with identical membership in every set of the family.
- **Superblocks** ([source](#pm-h-16)): Let $\mathcal G$ be the partition into atoms of $\{A:s_*(A)<S\}$; its parts will be called *superblocks*.
- **Cutting a part** ([source](#pm-h-16)): We say that $A$ cuts a part $D$ if both $A\cap D$ and $D\setminus A$ are nonempty.
- **Box-valid display** ([source](#pm-h-12)): A *box-valid display* is any integer vector in $\prod_i[0,m_i]$, with its total specified separately.
- **Summing at balance** ([source](#pm-h-12)): For two coordinates $a,b$ of the same capacity $M$, summing *at balance* means summing their values $(k,M-k)$, $0\le k\le M$.
- **Port** ([source](#pm-h-21)): A *port* is the part total $u_{iD}$, restricted to $[L_{iD},U_{iD}]\cap\mathbb Z$.
- **Addition property** ([source](#pm-h-30)): For a positive weight on all $r$-subsets of a finite set, the *addition property* means that, after any $r-2$ elements have been fixed included, the matrix of weights obtained by adding two distinct further elements, with zero diagonal, has at most one positive eigenvalue.
- **Omission property** ([source](#pm-h-30)): The *omission property* is the addition property of the complement-set weight.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-scale-n | N | = | $10^{10}+n+\ell+\lceil\varepsilon^{-1}\rceil$ | ground-set size, binary input length and inverse tolerance |
| q-scale-k | K | = | $N^5$ | function of N |
| q-scale-s | S | = | $N^{5000}$ | function of N |
| q-scale-w | W | = | $N^{20000}$ | function of N |
| q-scale-t | T | = | $W/N^{300}$ | function of W and N |
| q-distance-bound | distance from an approximately feasible point to Q in sup norm | ≤ | $10(n+1)^2b$ | additive violation at most b |
| q-center-slack | maximum of a nonnegative affine function over Q | ≤ | $4(n+1)F(c)$ | dimension n and rational center c |
| q-expansion-factor | expanded common-base count | ≤ | $e^{20N^{-8}}Z$ | function of N and Z |
| q-second-derivative | second derivative of the smoothed block log-integral | ≤ | $N^{800}/W^2$ | absolute value of each mixed second derivative |
| q-third-derivative | third derivative of the smoothed block log-integral | ≤ | $N^{1250}/W^3$ | absolute value of each mixed third derivative |
| q-weight-log-bound | absolute logarithm of the paired-slice weight | ≤ | $N^{60000}$ | function of N, independent of the numerical capacity |
| q-proxy-error | logarithmic error of the weighted port sum against the normalized count | ≤ | $100N^{-7}$ | function of N |
| q-reference-error | logarithmic error of the reference annealing estimate | ≤ | $4H_o\eta$ | number of phases and per-phase tolerance |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-oracle-tools** [claim](#stmt-prop-oracle-tools) / [proof](#stmt-prop-oracle-tools): Cite submodular minimization and the strong separation-optimization equivalence; clear denominators to keep encoding lengths polynomial. -> polynomial-cost separation and optimization over base polytopes
- **step-base-calculus** [claim](#stmt-lem-base-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-base-calculus): Greedy chains with decreasing marginal increments, summation by parts and support-function comparison. -> identities for sums, translations, reflections and projections of base polytopes
- **step-expansion-matroid** [claim](#stmt-lem-expansion-matroid) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-expansion-matroid): Verify matroid augmentation through tight sets closed under union and intersection, then compute rank by maximal independent subprofiles. -> expansion matroid with explicit rank formula
- **step-polyhedral-controls** [claim](#stmt-lem-polyhedral-controls) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-polyhedral-controls): Uncross tight sets into chains, apply row-signing total unimodularity, then use cofactor bounds for distance and basis affine dependence for slopes. -> vertex integrality, distance bound, integer-slope optimum function
- **step-center** [claim](#stmt-lem-center) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-center): Build a vertex simplex by linear optimization, then replace vertices whenever a barycentric coordinate exceeds the threshold. -> rational center and spanning simplex
- **step-balanced-summation** [claim](#stmt-lem-balanced-summation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-balanced-summation): Induction on one pair, finding a common codimension-one subspace on which the summed form is nonpositive; reflection gives the addition case. -> balanced sum keeps positivity and signatures
- **step-rank-signatures** [claim](#stmt-lem-rank-signatures) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rank-signatures): Use contractions of the expansion matroid, parallel classes and the dual matroid to write the normalized complement as a positive semidefinite block sum. -> normalized positive-semidefinite tests for rank factors
- **step-shift-logconcavity** [claim](#stmt-prop-shift-logconcavity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-shift-logconcavity): Encode the difference count as a balanced sum of rank factors, apply the removal signature to a two-by-two matrix, and pass to the limit. -> interval support and secant log-concavity of difference counts
- **step-generator-prefixes** [claim](#stmt-lem-generator-prefixes) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-generator-prefixes): Order atoms by decreasing membership sets and bound prefix slack by nonnegative submodularity. -> prefix slack bound and atom total range
- **step-partitions** [claim](#stmt-lem-partitions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-partitions): Minimize slack under pairwise inclusion and exclusion constraints to obtain short generating families. -> fine partitions, superblocks and compatible orders
- **step-shift-average** [claim](#stmt-lem-shift-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-shift-average): Show positivity for small shifts, use concavity of logarithms for slope bounds, then bound kernel tails. -> shift ratio and average comparison
- **step-refinement-witnesses** [claim](#stmt-lem-refinement-witnesses) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-refinement-witnesses): Iterative refinement by fixing totals on parts, choosing centers of boxed slices and using the center property and distance bound. -> terminal slices with witness points of large slack
- **step-expansion-cost** [claim](#stmt-prop-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-expansion): Compare lattice sums with cell integrals, contract expanded cells toward the witness by a homothety and bound the weight change. -> expanded count is within a small factor of the original count
- **step-tree-coordinates** [claim](#stmt-lem-tree-coordinates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tree-coordinates): Peel leaves of a spanning tree to solve for edge values from margins. -> integral unimodular parametrization
- **step-port-penalties** [claim](#stmt-lem-port-penalties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-port-penalties): Apply diminishing marginal returns along the order of each system to bound violations by block penalties. -> equivalence between penalties and expanded polytopes
- **step-smooth-derivatives** [claim](#stmt-lem-smooth-derivatives) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-smooth-derivatives): Bound total variation of kernel derivatives, subtract the integral and use the Lipschitz bound. -> second and third derivative bounds
- **step-block-signatures** [claim](#stmt-lem-block-signatures) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-block-signatures): Compare finite differences with the Hessian, let the quadratic term absorb the error and treat the absolute-value penalty as a scalar multiplier. -> normalized positive-semidefinite tests at every power
- **step-port-model** [claim](#stmt-prop-port-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-port-model): Combine the rank and block factor signatures on disjoint coordinate groups and compare the balanced mass with the count. -> positive paired port model
- **step-weight-bounds** [claim](#stmt-lem-weight-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-weight-bounds): Integrate over a cube near the center for the lower bound and use Lipschitz penalties of rank factors. -> magnitude and encoding bounds on weights
- **step-volume-lift** [claim](#stmt-lem-volume-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-volume-lift): Bound the pointwise gap between the exponential and the power, check separation through affine pieces and exhibit inner and outer balls. -> rational polytope whose volume approximates the block integral
- **step-weight-evaluator** [claim](#stmt-prop-weight-evaluator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-weight-evaluator): Round the polytope, run a capped volume algorithm with median amplification, clip outputs and take rational powers by bisection. -> bounded positive rational weight evaluator and normalizer approximation
- **step-counting-lifts** [claim](#stmt-lem-counting-lifts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-counting-lifts): Cancel factorial multiplicities on group-constant subspaces and compare the lifts on defect classes. -> omission and addition properties of the two lifts
- **step-counting-transport** [claim](#stmt-prop-counting-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-counting-transport): Verify the hypotheses of the companion transport and trace framework. -> variance, time-average and trace mixing bounds
- **step-counting-initialization** [claim](#stmt-lem-counting-initialization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-counting-initialization): Use the log bound on the weight to compare adjacent phases and compute exact initial defect masses. -> phase comparisons and exact initial multipliers
- **step-counting-reference** [claim](#stmt-prop-counting-reference) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-counting-reference): Bound cap failures by Markov, bound empirical errors by variance estimates and union bounds, and telescope the ratios. -> reference estimator with controlled error
- **step-counting-bits** [claim](#stmt-lem-counting-bits) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-counting-bits): Count elementary actions, couple the implementation to the reference experiment and bound multiplier sizes. -> deterministic resource bounds and coupling
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-counting-bits-2): Handle trivial branches exactly, combine the port estimate with the normalizer approximation, and amplify the success probability by a median of independent runs. -> uniform randomized approximation scheme with polynomial resource bounds

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-fprras-guarantee**: For every admissible input the single randomized algorithm returns a nonnegative rational estimate of the common-base count that lies within the stated relative error with the stated confidence.
- **inv-zero-count-exact**: When the set of common integer bases is empty the algorithm outputs zero on every execution, not only with high probability.
- **inv-center-slack**: A rational center of a nonempty bounded rational polytope controls the maximum of every nonnegative affine function on it by a dimension-dependent multiple of its value at the center.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-exact-count**: Using the output as an exact count or as an integrity check. -> The output is only a relative approximation that can fail with positive probability, so equality with the true count is not guaranteed.
- **anti-inexact-oracle**: Running the method with noisy or approximate rank evaluations, or without the stated finiteness of the ground set. -> Exact submodular minimization and the separation-optimization steps rely on exact value oracles, so inexact answers invalidate feasibility tests and the center construction.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main result is a randomized approximation scheme whose proof uses astronomically large fixed polynomial scales, an imported companion transport estimate and continuous volume approximation; the paper gives no small finite procedure whose output can be checked exactly against an independent oracle, and the guarantee is probabilistic, so a deterministic pass or fail test would not certify it.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a theoretical approximation scheme with very large fixed polynomial exponents, so the paper itself gives no concrete implementation. Using it as a drop-in library would be premature. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns game state, procedural content or physical simulation. Counting common integer bases of two polymatroids is not a simulation primitive. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper builds no tool or user-facing utility. It gives an existence-style polynomial bound whose cost is stated to come with large exponents. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The main theorem is a randomized approximation algorithm for counting common integer bases, with a multiplicative accuracy and failure probability guarantee and polynomial oracle and bit cost. The proof is not given as runnable pseudocode, so this is adaptable rather than directly implementable. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is proposed. Only a labelled-pair ground set of polynomial size and stored rational multipliers appear, as internal bookkeeping. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper makes no statement about parallel hardware, memory layout or circuits. The work is dominated by oracle calls and exact rational arithmetic with no hardware angle. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The scheme gives relative-error estimates with nonzero failure probability, so it cannot provide exact counts or integrity guarantees. No security notion is defined. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning problem is posed. Annealing weights and multipliers adapted from samples serve counting only and carry no learning guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The input systems model capacitated allocation, and exact feasibility and optimization on the intersection are used as subroutines. The result counts feasible allocations rather than optimizing, so the link is indirect. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no distributed, concurrent or fault-tolerance content. All computation is a single randomized procedure with an oracle. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Relevant mathematical uses are within combinatorics, for example counting contingency tables, and are outside the fixed engineering list. |
| other (combinatorial_counting) | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The result approximately counts common integer bases, which includes counting cell-bounded contingency tables with binary-encoded bounds, a combinatorial counting setting. It is theory-driven, so use as a reference rather than a drop-in routine. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-exact-oracles** (hypothesis): The oracle algorithms, the feasibility test and the center construction require exact value oracles for the rank functions on a finite ground set, with total rank given in binary. Noisy or approximate rank answers are outside the stated setting. Blocked use: ['running the scheme with noisy or approximate rank evaluations', 'applying it to an infinite ground set']
- **limit-two-systems-equal-rank** (hypothesis): The result concerns exactly two normalized, nondecreasing, submodular rank functions with equal total rank. Integrality and the signature arguments are developed for the intersection of two base polytopes, or a base polytope with part-total equations. Blocked use: ['counting under three or more simultaneous polymatroid constraint systems', 'applying it when the two total ranks differ']
- **limit-approximate-output** (approximation): The output is a nonnegative rational estimate with relative error and failure probability governed by the tolerances, not an exact count. Only the case of no common base is answered exactly, by outputting zero on every execution. Blocked use: ['using the estimate as an exact count', 'using it as an integrity or audit check']
- **limit-large-exponents** (formal_scope): The polynomial bound holds with large fixed exponents; the paper states that the issue is dependence on the input encoding, not an optimized running time. Blocked use: ['treating the scheme as a practical production counter without reducing its exponents']
- **limit-refinement-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The refinement into slices with witness points is an existence argument used only for comparison; its subdivisions need not be polynomially many and are never enumerated by the algorithm. Blocked use: ['implementing the slice subdivision as a runnable stage', 'reading its witness points as outputs of the algorithm']
- **limit-companion-transport** (formal_scope): The transport, observable and trace-mixing estimates are imported from the companion framework; the stage is valid only under positivity, both lift properties, agreement on transversals, the comparison parameter and good multipliers. Blocked use: ['applying the trace-mixing bound when the weights lack either lift property', 'applying it with multipliers that are not good']
- **limit-volume-evaluation** (approximation): Weight evaluation relies on a randomized volume algorithm with capped resources; failed or interrupted runs are replaced by fixed positive rationals, so accuracy holds only on the success event. Blocked use: ['assuming every individual weight evaluation is accurate', 'dropping the caps and median amplification']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme for counting common integer bases of two polymatroids with the same total rank, supplied by exact rank-value oracles. The total rank and capacities are encoded in binary, and each integer vector is counted once. On every execution, the number of oracle calls and the bit work outside the oracles are bounded by a fixed polynomial in the ground-set size, the binary input length, the inverse relative-error tolerance, and the logarithm of the inverse failure probability. The algorithm handles binary capacities directly, without expanding them into labelled copies.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

An integral polymatroid describes nonnegative integer allocations subject to submodular capacity constraints. Intersecting two such systems is a natural common generalization of matroid intersection and capacitated transportation problems. Exact feasibility and optimization are accessible through rank-value oracles. Counting the feasible integer allocations poses additional difficulties: a large capacity permits exponentially many coordinate values, and replacing it by labelled copies changes both the size of the instance and the weight assigned to each allocation.

This paper gives an approximation algorithm whose cost depends polynomially on the binary encoding of the capacities. The proof builds on the common-matroid-base counting framework and its two-lift extension for cell-bounded contingency tables (OpenAI 2026b, 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Parent](#ref-Parent), [CB](#ref-CB)]. The new reduction keeps only part totals with polynomially many possible values as discrete variables. It treats the remaining degrees of freedom by weighted volume approximation and proves the discrete quadratic inequalities needed by the counting framework after this integration.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and the result

Let $E=\{1,\ldots,n\}$. For $i\in\{1,2\}$, let $r_i:2^E\to\mathbb Z_{\ge0}$ be normalized, nondecreasing, and submodular: $$r_i(\varnothing)=0,\qquad
 r_i(A)\le r_i(B)\quad(A\subseteq B),\qquad
 r_i(A)+r_i(B)\ge r_i(A\cup B)+r_i(A\cap B).$$ Assume their total ranks agree, and write $R=r_1(E)=r_2(E)$. For $x\in\mathbb R^E$ and $A\subseteq E$, put $x(A)=\sum_{j\in A}x_j$. The set of common integer bases is <a id="eq:counting-problem"></a>$$\label{eq:counting-problem}
 \Omega(r_1,r_2)=
 \left\{x\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^E:
 x(E)=R,\quad x(A)\le r_i(A)
 \text{ for every }A\subseteq E,\ i\in\{1,2\}\right\}.$$ Write $Z=\lvert\Omega(r_1,r_2)\rvert$. Every vector has weight one. A representation of a vector by labelled copies is not another object to be counted.

The input supplies $R$ in binary and exact value oracles for $r_1,r_2$. A query is an $n$-bit subset indicator; its answer is the binary integer $r_i(A)$, between zero and $R$. No decomposition, explicit inequality list, sampler, or counting oracle is part of the input. Let $\ell$ be the binary encoding length of $n,R,\varepsilon,\delta$, where the rational numbers $0<\varepsilon,\delta<1$ are given by their numerators and denominators. Oracle computation itself is excluded from the bit-operation count, but writing queries and reading answers are included.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a single uniform randomized algorithm which, for every input described above, outputs a nonnegative rational $\widehat Z$ satisfying $$\mathbb P\bigl((1-\varepsilon)Z\le\widehat Z\le(1+\varepsilon)Z\bigr)
 \ge1-\delta.$$ If $Z=0$, the algorithm outputs zero on every execution. On every execution, both its number of oracle calls and its number of other bit operations are bounded by one fixed polynomial in $$n,\qquad \ell,\qquad \varepsilon^{-1},\qquad \log(\delta^{-1}).$$ The bit-operation bound includes rational arithmetic, generation of independent unbiased random bits, and the encoding of the output.*

</div>

The theorem includes lower-dimensional feasible sets, arbitrary pairs of rank functions, and binary ranks of unrestricted magnitude. When $R=0$, there is one common base, the zero vector; this includes the empty vector when $n=0$. The algorithm’s polynomial has large fixed exponents. The issue here is dependence on the input encoding, not an optimized running time.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Context and prior work

The polyhedral theory begins with Edmonds’ work on submodular functions, matroids, and polymatroid intersection (Edmonds 1970) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Edmonds1970](#ref-Edmonds1970)]. It explains why the intersection considered here is integral and why optimization admits an oracle formulation. The exact algorithms used below come from submodular minimization (Iwata, Fleischer, and Fujishige 2001) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[IFF2001](#ref-IFF2001)] and the rational separation–optimization theory of Grötschel, Lovász, and Schrijver (Grötschel, Lovász, and Schrijver 1988) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GLS1988](#ref-GLS1988)]. The representation of an integer polymatroid by a matroid on groups of interchangeable elements also has a classical history, in Helgason’s theory of hypermatroids (Helgason 1974) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Helgason1974](#ref-Helgason1974)]. This representation preserves the feasible occupancy vectors, but its explicit size and the number of labelled realizations of a vector matter for counting. The distinction becomes essential when capacities are encoded in binary.

Contingency tables provide a concrete instance of this distinction. Such a table is a nonnegative integer matrix with prescribed row and column sums; cell bounds impose additional upper bounds on its entries. Dyer, Kannan, and Mount obtained approximate counting and sampling algorithms under lower bounds on the margins, using the relation between lattice counts and transportation-polytope volumes (Dyer, Kannan, and Mount 1997) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DKM1997](#ref-DKM1997)]. Cryan and Dyer gave an FPRAS with arbitrary margins when the number of rows is fixed (Cryan and Dyer 2003) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[CD2003](#ref-CD2003)], and Cryan, Dyer, and Randall extended that fixed-row result to cell-bounded tables (Cryan, Dyer, and Randall 2010) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[CDR2010](#ref-CDR2010)]. For fixed-row tables without cell bounds, Gopalan et al. subsequently gave a deterministic FPTAS (Gopalan et al. 2011, Theorem 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GKM2011](#ref-GKM2011)]. These results already address numerical data through their binary encodings, within their respective restrictions. For tables with $0/1$ entries, Bezáková, Bhatnagar, and Vigoda gave an annealing-based FPRAS for arbitrary margins (Bezáková, Bhatnagar, and Vigoda 2007) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BBV2007](#ref-BBV2007)]. The companion manuscript (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CB](#ref-CB)] treats cell-bounded tables with both dimensions variable. Its paired encoding and two factorial corrections are the immediate counting tools used here; the present reduction must additionally handle arbitrary submodular rank constraints available only through value queries.

Another line of work develops the concavity behind matroid counting. Adiprasito, Huh, and Katz proved the Hodge–Riemann relations for matroid Chow rings and deduced log concavity of characteristic-polynomial coefficients (Adiprasito, Huh, and Katz 2018) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[AHK2018](#ref-AHK2018)]. Anari, Liu, Oveis Gharan, and Vinzant used log-concave polynomials and high-dimensional walks to obtain an independence-oracle FPRAS for the bases of a single arbitrary matroid (Anari et al. 2024) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ALOV2024](#ref-ALOV2024)]. Brändén and Huh’s theory of Lorentzian polynomials includes matroid basis polynomials and factorial-normalized generating polynomials of integer polymatroid bases (Brändén and Huh 2020) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BH2020](#ref-BH2020)]. These statements concern an individual matroid or polymatroid; taking common bases need not preserve the exchange property of its support. Moreover, the factorial normalization weights integer bases nonuniformly. They therefore do not directly yield the uniform common-base count considered here. Our proof verifies the needed quadratic inequalities for its particular auxiliary weights.

For common bases of two rank-$r$ matroids, Anari, Oveis Gharan, and Vinzant obtained a deterministic polynomial-time $2^{O(r)}$-approximation and a randomized relative approximation with running time $2^{O(r)}\operatorname{poly}(n,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\delta^{-1})$ (Anari, Oveis Gharan, and Vinzant 2021, Theorem 1.3 and Corollary 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[AOV2021](#ref-AOV2021)]. The companion manuscript (OpenAI 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Parent](#ref-Parent)] gives an FPRAS for common bases of two arbitrary matroids on an explicitly listed ground set. It uses positive rank-deficiency weights, transport of a single defect, variance bounds for specified observables, and mixing of a trace chain. The cell-bounded companion (OpenAI 2026a) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CB](#ref-CB)] develops the two complementary factorial lifts that cancel labelled multiplicities on the target states. These constructions are related to the perfect- and near-perfect matching ensemble and learned defect weights in the permanent FPRAS of Jerrum, Sinclair, and Vigoda (Jerrum, Sinclair, and Vigoda 2004) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[JSV2004](#ref-JSV2004)]. Restricted Poincaré inequalities for the hole-pattern observables identifying unmatched vertices also appear in the recent permanent algorithm of Chen, Vigoda, and Yang (Chen, Vigoda, and Yang 2026) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[CVY2026](#ref-CVY2026)]. The formal transport and counting inputs to this paper are the specific statements from the two companions; our contribution is the reduction to an instance whose explicit size is polynomial in the original binary input.

The continuous part uses two classical results with different roles. Prékopa’s marginal theorem preserves log concavity when free coordinates are integrated out (Prékopa 1973, Theorem 6) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Prekopa1973](#ref-Prekopa1973)]. The randomized convex-volume algorithm of Dyer, Frieze, and Kannan (Dyer, Frieze, and Kannan 1991) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[DFK1991](#ref-DFK1991)], in the rational oracle formulation described by Dyer and Frieze (Dyer and Frieze 1991) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DyerFrieze1991](#ref-DyerFrieze1991)], supplies approximate evaluations of the resulting weights. Our rational-center construction uses determinant replacement, closely related to the barycentric-spanner construction of Awerbuch and Kleinberg (Awerbuch and Kleinberg 2004, Proposition 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[AK2004](#ref-AK2004)]; we give the affine simplex argument needed here. These continuous tools supply the weights and their evaluations, while the discrete signature argument establishes the additional inequalities required by the counting chain.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The first obstacle is the scale of the coordinate ranges. The expansion matroid of an integral polymatroid can represent each unit of capacity by a separate element, but a direct construction can have size proportional to $nR$. In addition, an occupancy vector has a product of binomial coefficients worth of labelled realizations. Applying a common-base counter to that expansion would therefore address neither the required runtime nor the required weights.

We begin with an exact feasibility test and a rational center of the common base polytope. Near-tight rank inequalities at this center define two partitions of $E$. Their part totals will be the discrete variables retained by the algorithm. A coarser partition identifies directions in which pairs of bases can be shifted without significantly changing their count. Directional log concavity makes this comparison quantitative. Within suitable fixed-total slices, a small homothety then shows that enlarging the original base polytopes in the remaining directions changes the count by only a small relative error.

For the retained part totals, an incidence graph joins a part of the first partition to a part of the second whenever they share a coordinate. Choosing spanning trees expresses all other coordinates through free variables and the part totals. The retained integer ranges have polynomial cardinality. Large free ranges are integrated out, with soft penalties for rank violations. The preceding comparison proves that the resulting weighted count approximates $Z$.

Integration preserves ordinary log concavity, but that property alone does not give the discrete quadratic inequalities used by the counting algorithm. We convolve the integrated weights with a smooth compactly supported kernel and add a small strictly concave quadratic term. Derivative estimates bound the errors between discrete second differences and the continuous Hessian. The added curvature dominates these errors at every annealing power, giving both required quadratic tests throughout the counting algorithm.

Finally, each bounded part-total variable is represented by a polynomial number of paired labels. Two factorially corrected lifts agree on transversals, where their multiplicities cancel exactly, and satisfy the complementary signature conditions of the paired counting framework. We state the imported transport and trace estimates precisely, verify their hypotheses, and give the capped annealing algorithm. A separate convex-volume construction evaluates its weights with bounded rational computation. This last distinction is essential: all large expansions and slice subdivisions used for comparison are proof devices, whereas every instance actually sampled has polynomial size.

Section <a href="#sec:polyhedra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:polyhedra">2</a> supplies the oracle and polyhedral tools. Section <a href="#sec:signatures" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signatures">3</a> proves the integer signature and shift-count statements. Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">4</a> establishes the geometric comparison, and Section <a href="#sec:ports" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:ports">5</a> constructs the regularized polynomial-range model. Section <a href="#sec:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:evaluation">6</a> evaluates its weights. Section <a href="#sec:counting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:counting">7</a> performs the paired counting and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:polyhedra"></a>
### Polyhedral tools and a feasible center

The first step is to work with the exponentially many rank inequalities without enumerating them. We record the exact oracle algorithms we use, then establish the geometric estimates needed to replace large coordinate ranges by short ranges of part totals. In particular, the distance estimate below depends on the dimension, not on the numerical value of the rank.

Recall that for $x\in\mathbb R^E$ and $A\subseteq E$ we write $x(A)=\sum_{j\in A}x_j$. A set function $f:2^E\to\mathbb R$ is *normalized* if $f(\varnothing)=0$. For any normalized submodular function, including one that is not nondecreasing, put <a id="eq:base-polytope"></a>$$\label{eq:base-polytope}
 B(f)=\{x\in\mathbb R^E:x(E)=f(E),\quad x(A)\leq f(A)
                 \text{ for every }A\subseteq E\}.$$ This convention lets us translate and reflect base polytopes. The singleton and complementary-singleton inequalities give $$f(E)-f(E\setminus\{j\})\leq x_j\leq f(\{j\})
 \qquad (x\in B(f)).$$ Thus $B(f)$ is bounded; for a nondecreasing $f$, its coordinates are nonnegative. Throughout, integrals in free coordinates use ordinary Lebesgue measure with unit lattice mesh. An integral in zero coordinates means evaluation of its integrand.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Exact oracle operations

We use the following standard algorithmic results. Their encoding requirements are relevant because the capacity $R$ is given in binary.

<a id="stmt-prop-oracle-tools"></a>
<div id="prop:oracle-tools" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2** (Oracle algorithms). *The following operations have deterministic oracle and bit complexity bounded by fixed polynomials in the indicated input sizes.*

1.  *An integer-valued submodular function on a finite ground set can be minimized, with a minimizing set returned, from an exact value oracle and a bound on the binary lengths of its values. The size parameters are the ground-set cardinality and that value-length bound.*

2.  *Let $Q\subseteq\mathbb R^d$ be a bounded rational polytope, supplied by strong separation, a coordinate bound, and a bound on the binary encoding length of each individual defining inequality. The separator returns inequalities of length polynomial in the query length and the supplied description bounds. Exact rational linear optimization, including an emptiness decision and the production of an optimal vertex when $Q$ is nonempty, is polynomial in $d$, these bounds, and the objective length. This assertion includes lower-dimensional polytopes. All separation queries have polynomial encoding length.*

*The first operation also applies to an integer submodular function with a rational modular term added, and with prescribed elements required to belong or not to belong to the minimizing set.*

</div>
